I might lose track of what they’re saying sometimes, but interrupting or blurting things out isn’t something I’ve noticed as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. It’s more that if I wasn’t fully paying attention at the time, then later I might not have much to recall.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t have a clear example in mind of being especially bad at organizing tasks or managing time. I use lists and calendars like most people, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, such as noise or other people talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. If people are talking nearby, I may have to refocus, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or that your thinking is cloudy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a fog. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. Sometimes I may feel a little mentally tired, but not like I’m in a fog, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having a hard time staying focused, especially at work. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has completely drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ve missed the main point of what people were discussing. It’s been frustrating enough that I felt like I needed to get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about two years and four months now. It really started after I got promoted and my workload got bigger—my usual ways of keeping things organized just weren’t enough anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still. I might shift around a little like anyone does, but restlessness or fidgeting hasn’t been the main issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’m usually pretty careful about letting people finish. I might lose track of what they’re saying sometimes, but interrupting or blurting things out isn’t something I’ve noticed as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. It’s more that if I wasn’t fully paying attention at the time, then later I might not have much to recall.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t have a clear example in mind of being especially bad at organizing tasks or managing time. I use lists and calendars like most people, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, such as noise or other people talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. If people are talking nearby, I may have to refocus, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or that your thinking is cloudy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a fog. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. If something is complicated it may take me a little time, but I wouldn’t say it stands out as a specific issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things like appointments, deadlines, or tasks that you need to complete?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do forget to follow through on tasks sometimes. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ve missed the main point of what people were discussing. It’s been frustrating enough that I felt like I needed to get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about two years and four months now. It really started after I got promoted and my workload got bigger—my usual ways of keeping things organized just weren’t enough anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. I don’t have a clear example in mind of being especially bad at organizing tasks or managing time. I use lists and calendars like most people, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, such as noise or other people talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. If people are talking nearby, I may have to refocus, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or that your thinking is cloudy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a fog. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’m having a hard time staying focused, especially at work. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has completely drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ve missed the main point of what people were discussing. It’s been frustrating enough that I felt like I needed to get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about two years and four months now. It really started after I got promoted and my workload got bigger—my usual ways of keeping things organized just weren’t enough anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still. I might shift around a little like anyone does, but restlessness or fidgeting hasn’t been the main issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’m usually pretty careful about letting people finish. I might lose track of what they’re saying sometimes, but interrupting or blurting things out isn’t something I’ve noticed as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. It’s more that if I wasn’t fully paying attention at the time, then later I might not have much to recall.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t have a clear example in mind of being especially bad at organizing tasks or managing time. I use lists and calendars like most people, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, such as noise or other people talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. If people are talking nearby, I may have to refocus, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or that your thinking is cloudy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a fog. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. Sometimes I may feel a little mentally tired, but not like I’m in a fog, at least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel mentally tired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often.” I get tired like anyone does, especially after a long day, but I haven’t really noticed mental tiredness as a major ongoing thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with learning new things?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed a problem learning new things, no. If something is complicated it may take me a little time, but I wouldn’t say it stands out as a specific issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things like appointments, deadlines, or tasks that you need to complete?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do forget to follow through on tasks sometimes. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four next steps, I might do the first one and maybe start the second, but then the email or spreadsheet or whatever final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with making decisions?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can go back and forth on things if there are a lot of options, but I wouldn’t say making decisions is a major difficulty for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main thing. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report or be in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has wandered. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I notice I missed the point of what people were discussing.\n\nDoctor: When you find your mind has wandered and you have to reread something, do you feel like you didn't understand it the first time or that you just forgot what you read?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I just didn’t take it in. So, yes, it’s affecting my quality of life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty with focus and attention gets worse after you've been working on something for a while?\n\nPatient: Yes. I notice it can happen pretty quickly, actually. I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit in a clinic metrics meeting intending to stay with it, and after a few minutes my mind has drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report, or I’ll be in a clinic metrics meeting trying to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has completely drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I’ve missed the main point of what people were discussing. It’s been frustrating enough that I felt like I needed to get it checked out.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: It’s been going on for about two years and four months now. It really started after I got promoted and my workload got bigger—my usual ways of keeping things organized just weren’t enough anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don’t think of myself as someone who can’t sit still. I’m usually pretty careful about letting people finish. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. It’s more that if I wasn’t fully paying attention at the time, then later I might not have much to recall.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time effectively?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that. I don’t have a clear example in mind of being especially bad at organizing tasks or managing time. I use lists and calendars like most people, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you, such as noise or other people talking?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. If people are talking nearby, I may have to refocus, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a fog or that your thinking is cloudy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a fog. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear problem with remembering recent things specifically. I mean, normal background noise can be distracting sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed it as a constant or major problem. I don’t really notice my thinking feeling cloudy in that way. Like after a committee meeting, if someone gives me three or four next steps, I might do the first one and maybe start the second, but then the email or spreadsheet or whatever final submission just sits there unfinished until someone reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with making decisions?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can go back and forth on things if there are a lot of options, but I wouldn’t say making decisions is a major difficulty for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main thing. I’ll sit down to work on an EPIC quality report or be in a clinic metrics meeting, fully intending to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has wandered. Then I’m rereading the same numbers over and over, or I notice I missed the point of what people were discussing.\n\nDoctor: When you find your mind has wandered and you have to reread something, do you feel like you didn't understand it the first time or that you just forgot what you read?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I just didn’t take it in. I may have looked at the numbers or heard the discussion, but my mind drifted, so when I come back to it I’m rereading because I realize I missed the point, not because it was too hard to understand.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus and attention affecting your relationships or overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Mostly at work, I’ve had delays with clinic reporting deadlines, and that ends up making other teams wait on me. That feels pretty bad, because I know it affects more than just my own schedule.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve had to step back from leading parts of the volunteer health workshops because I couldn’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving. So, yes, it’s affecting my quality of life in a real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty with focus and attention gets worse after you've been working on something for a while?\n\nPatient: Yes. I notice it can happen pretty quickly, actually. I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit in a clinic metrics meeting intending to stay with it, and after a few minutes my mind has drifted. Then I’m rereading the same numbers or realizing I missed the main point of the discussion.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when working on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s “often.” I do make mistakes sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked whether they’re careless mistakes versus just normal errors. I’d have to think about that more.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing difficulties with focus and attention every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much every day. It’s not every single minute, but it happens during routine work things. I’ll start an EPIC quality report or sit in a clinic metrics meeting meaning to follow along, and within a few minutes I realize my mind has wandered. Then I’m rereading the same numbers or trying to catch up because I missed the main point.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble with word-finding, like being unable to think of the right word when speaking or writing?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll occasionally blank on a word like anyone does, but I haven’t noticed that being a particular problem for me when I’m speaking or writing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
